Wavenumber-explicit h ​ p hp -FEM analysis for Maxwell’s equations with impedance boundary conditions
J.M. Melenk, S.A. Sauter

Abstract
The time-harmonic Maxwell equations at high wavenumber k k in domains with an analytic boundary and impedance boundary conditions are considered. A wavenumber-explicit stability and regularity theory is developed that decomposes the solution into a part with finite Sobolev regularity that is controlled uniformly in k k and an analytic part. Using this regularity, quasi-optimality of the Galerkin discretization based on Nédélec elements of order p p on a mesh with mesh size h h is shown under the k k -explicit scale resolution condition that a) k ​ h / p kh/p is sufficient small and b) p / ln ⁡ k p/\ln k is bounded from below.

1
Introduction

The time-harmonic Maxwell equations at high wavenumber
k
are a fundamental
component of high-frequency computational electromagnetics. Computationally,
these equations are challenging for several reasons. The solutions are highly
oscillatory so that fine discretizations are necessary and correspondingly
large computational resources are required. While conditions to resolve the
oscillatory nature of the solution appear unavoidable, even more stringent
conditions on the discretizations have to be imposed for stability reasons: In
many numerical methods based on the variational formulation of Maxwell’s
equations, the gap between the actual error and the best approximation error
widens as the wavenumber
k
becomes large. This “pollution
effect” is a manifestation of a lack of coercivity of the
problem, as is typical in time-harmonic wave propagation problems.
Mathematically understanding this “pollution
effect” in terms of the wavenumber
k
and the
discretization parameters for the model problem (
1.1
) is the
purpose of the present work.

The “pollution effect”, i.e., the fact that
discretizations of time-harmonic wave propagation problems are prone to
dispersion errors, is probably best studied for the Helmholtz equation at
large wavenumbers. The beneficial effect of using high order methods was
numerically observed very early and substantiated for translation-invariant
meshes
[
Ain04a
,
Ain04b
]
; a rigorous mathematical analysis for
unstructured meshes was developed in the last decade only in

[
MS10
,
MS11
,
EM12
]
. These works
analyze high order FEM (
hp
-FEM) for the Helmholtz equation in a Gårding
setting using duality techniques. This technique, often called
“Schatz argument”, crucially hinges on the
regularity of the dual problem, which is again a Helmholtz problem. The key
new insight of the line of work
[
MS10
,
MS11
,
EM12
]
is a refined wavenumber-explicit regularity theory for
Helmholtz problems that takes the following form (“regularity
by decomposition”): given data, the solution
u
is written
as
u_{H^{2}}+u_{\mathcal{A}}
where
u_{H^{2}}
has the regularity expected
of elliptic problems and is controlled in terms of the data with constants

independent
of
k
. The part
u_{\mathcal{A}}
is a (piecewise)
analytic function whose regularity is described explicitly in terms of
k
.
Employing “regularity by decomposition” for
the analysis of discretizations has been successfully applied to other
Helmholtz problems and discretizations such DG methods
[
MPS13
]
, BEM

[
LM11
]
, FEM-BEM coupling

[
MMPR20
]
, and heterogeneous Helmholtz problems

[
CFN20
,
BCFM22
,
LSW22
,
LSW21
]
.

In this paper, we consider the following time-harmonic Maxwell equations with
impedance boundary conditions as our model problem:

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{curl}{\mathbf{E}}-k^{2}{\mathbf{E}}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{f}}\quad\mbox{in $\Omega$},

(1.1a)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{curl}{\mathbf{E}})\times{\mathbf{n}}-\operatorname{i}k{\mathbf{E}}_{T}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{g}}_{T}\quad\mbox{on $\partial\Omega$}

(1.1b)

on a bounded Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with simply
connected boundary
\partial\Omega
. We study an
{\mathbf{H}}(\operatorname{curl})
-conforming Galerkin method with elements of degree
p

on a mesh of size
h
and show quasi-optimality of the method under
the

scale resolution condition

\frac{\left|k\right|h}{p}\leq c_{1}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad p\geq c_{2}\ln\left|k\right|,

(1.2)

where
c_{2}
>0

is arbitrary and
c_{1}
>0

is sufficiently small (Theorem
9.7
). The resolution
condition
\left|k\right|h/p\leq c_{1}
is a natural condition to
resolve the oscillatory behavior of the solution, and the side constraint

p\geq c_{2}\ln\left|k\right|
is a rather weak condition that
suppresses the “pollution effect”.

Compared to the scalar Helmholtz case, where the compact
embedding
H^{1}\subset L^{2}
underlies the success of the duality argument,
the convergence analysis of discretizations of Maxwell’s equations is
hampered by the fact that the embedding

\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl})\subset\mathbf{L}^{2}
is not compact so
that a duality argument is not immediately applicable. This issue arises
even in the context of convergence analyses that are not explicit in the
wavenumber
k
. An analysis can be based on the observation that

\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl})\cap\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{div})
endowed with appropriate boundary conditions
is compactly embedded in
\mathbf{L}^{2}
. This approach, which
is structurally described in
[
MS21a
, Sec. 1.2]
,
involves as a first ingredient the ability to decompose discrete functions
into gradient parts and (discrete) solenoidal parts in two ways, namely,
on the continuous level and the discrete level. The solenoidal part of
the decomposition on the continuous level
is in

\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl})\cap\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{div})

and admits a duality argument.
Galerkin orthogonalities are invoked to then reduce the analysis to that
of the difference between the solenoidal parts of the continuous
and the discrete level. For the analysis of this difference,
a second ingredient is vital, namely, special interpolation operators
with a commuting diagram property. These two ingredients underlie
many duality arguments for Maxwell problems in the literature, see,
e.g.,
[
Mon03
, Sec. 7.2]
,

[
ZSWX09
,
CFNP18
,
EG18
,
CF19
]
and references therein. The present work
follows
[
Mon03
, Sec. 7.2]
and the path outlined in

[
MS21a
, Sec. 1.1–1.3]
.

At the heart of the
k
-explicit convergence analysis for
(
1.1
) is a
k
-explicit regularity theory for the above
mentioned dual problem. Similarly to the Helmholtz case discussed
above, it takes the form of a “regularity by decomposition”
(Theorem
7.3
).
Such a regularity theory was developed for
Maxwell’s equations in full space in
the recent
paper
[
MS21a
]
, where the decomposition is directly
accessible in terms of the Newton potential and layer potentials.
For the present
bounded domain case, however, an explicit construction of the decomposition is
not available, and the iterative construction as in the Helmholtz case of

[
MS11
]
has to be brought to bear. For this, a significant
complication in the Maxwell case compared to the Helmholtz case arises from
the requirement that the frequency filters used in the construction be such
that they produce solenoidal fields if the argument is solenoidal.

While our wavenumber-explicit regularity result Theorem
7.3

underlies our proof of quasioptimal convergence of the high order Galerkin
method (cf. Theorem
9.7
), it also proves useful for
wavenumber-explicit interpolation error estimates as worked out in
Corollary
9.8
.

The present paper analyzes an
{\mathbf{H}}(\operatorname{curl})
-conforming
discretization based on high order Nédélec elements. Various other
high order methods for Maxwell’s equations that are explicit in the wavenumber
can be found in the literature. Closest to our work are

[
NT20
,
CFV22
]
. The work

[
NT20
]
studies the same problem (
1.1
) but uses an

{\mathbf{H}}^{1}
-based instead of an
{\mathbf{H}}(\operatorname{curl})
-based variational formulation involving both the electric and the magnetic
field. The proof of quasi-optimality in
[
NT20
]
is based on a
“regularity by decomposition” technique
similar to the present one.
[
NT19
]
studies the same

{\mathbf{H}}^{1}
-based variational formulation and
{\mathbf{H}}^{1}
-conforming discretizations for (
1.1
) on certain polyhedral
domains and obtains
k
-explicit conditions on the discretization for
quasi-optimality. Key to this is a description of the solution regularity in

[
NT19
]
in terms
of corner and edge singularities. The work
[
CFV22
]

studies fixed (but arbitrary)
order
{\mathbf{H}}(\operatorname{curl})
-conforming discretizations of
heterogeneous Maxwell problems and shows a similar quasi-optimality result by
generalizing the corresponding Helmholtz result

[
CFN20
]
; the restriction to finite order methods
compared to the present work appears to be due to the difference in which the
decomposition of solutions of Maxwell problems is obtained. High order
Discontinuous Galerkin (DG) and Hybridizable DG (HDG) methods for
(
1.1
) have been presented in
[
FW14
]
and

[
LCQ17
]
together with a stability analysis that is explicit in

h
,
k
, and
p
. A dispersion analysis of high order methods on
tensor-product meshes is given in
[
Ain04b
]
.

The outline of the paper is as follows. Section
2
introduces
the notation and tools such as regular decompositions (see
Section
2.4
) that are indispensable for the
analysis of Maxwell problems. Section
3

(Theorem
3.7
) shows that the solution of (
1.1
)
depends only polynomially on the wavenumber
k
. This stability result is
obtained using layer potential techniques in the spirit of earlier work

[
EM12
, Thm. 2.4]
for the analogous Helmholtz equation.
While earlier stability estimates for (
1.1
) in

[
HMP11
,
FW14
,
Ver19
]
, and
[
NT19
, Thm. 5.2]
are obtained by judicious choices of test functions and
rely on star-shapedness of the geometry, Theorem
3.7
does
not require star-shapedness. It is worth mentioning that at least
in the analogous case of the Helmholtz equation, alternatives to the
use of suitable test functions or layer potential exist, which can lead
to better
k
-dependencies; we refer to
[
Spe14
]
for results and
a discussion.
Section
4
analyzes a
“sign definite” Maxwell problem and
presents
k
-explicit regularity assertions for it
(Theorem
4.3
). The motivation for studying this
particular boundary value problem is that, since the principal parts of our
sign-definite Maxwell operator and that of (
1.1
) coincide, a
contraction argument can be brought to bear in the proof of
Theorem
7.3
. A similar technique has recently been used for
heterogeneous Helmholtz problems in
[
BCFM22
]
.
Section
5
collects
k
-explicit regularity assertions for
(
1.1
) (Lemma
5.1
for finite regularity data
and Theorem
5.2
for analytic data). The contraction argument in
the proof of Theorem
7.3
relies on certain frequency splitting
operators (both in the volume and on the boundary), which are provided in
Section
6
. Section
7
presents the main analytical
result, Theorem
7.3
, where the solution of (
1.1
) with
finite regularity data
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
is decomposed into a part
with finite regularity but
k
-uniform bounds, a gradient field, and an
analytic part. Section
8
presents the discretization of
(
1.1
) based on high order Nédélec elements
and presents
hp
-approximation operators that map into
Nédélec spaces. These operators are the same ones as used
in
[
MS21a
]
but we work out their approximation properties
on the skeleton of the mesh since stronger approximation properties on the boundary

\partial\Omega
are required in the present case of impedance boundary
conditions.
Section
9
shows quasi-optimality
(Theorem
9.7
) under the scale resolution condition
(
1.2
). Section
10
concludes the paper
with numerical results.

2
Setting

2.1
Geometric setting and Sobolev spaces on Lipschitz
domains

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain which we
assume throughout the paper to have a simply connected and sufficiently smooth
boundary
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
; if less regularity is required, we will
specify this. We flag already at this point that the main quasi-optimal
convergence result, Theorem
9.7
will require
analyticity of
\Gamma
. The outward unit normal vector field is denoted by

\mathbf{n}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{2}
.

The Maxwell problem in the frequency domain involves the wavenumber (denoted
by
k
) and we assume that
1
1

1

We exclude here a neighborhood of
0

since we are interested in the high-frequency behavior – to simplify notation
we have fixed
k_{0}=1
while any other positive choice
k_{0}\in\left(0,1\right)
leads to qualitatively the same results while constants then
depend continuously on
k_{0}\in\left(0,1\right)
and, possibly,
deteriorate as
k_{0}\rightarrow 0
.

k\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left(-k_{0},k_{0}\right)\quad\text{for }k_{0}=1.

(2.1)

Let
L^{2}(\Omega)
denote the usual Lebesgue space on
\Omega
with scalar
product
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
and norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}:=\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{1/2}
.
Recall that the complex conjugation is applied to the second argument in
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
. If the domain
\Omega
is clear from
the context we write short
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
,
\left\|\cdot\right\|
for
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{L^{2}\left(\Omega\right)}
,
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
.
For Sobolev spaces, we follow the notation of
[
McL00
]
.
For
s\geq 0
we denote
by
H^{s}(\Omega)
the usual Sobolev spaces of index
s
with norm

\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{s}\left(\Omega\right)}

and by
\widetilde{H}^{s}(\Omega)=H^{s}_{\overline{\Omega}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})

the space of Sobolev functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with support in
\overline{\Omega}
.
For
s\geq 0
,
H^{-s}(\Omega)
denotes the dual of
\widetilde{H}^{s}(\Omega)
.
The space
\mathbf{H}^{s}(\Omega)
of vector-valued functions is characterized by
componentwise membership in
H^{s}(\Omega)
.
We write
(\cdot,\cdot)
also for the vectorial
\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)
inner
product given by
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})=\int_{\Omega}\langle\mathbf{f},\overline{\mathbf{g}}\rangle
.
Here, we introduce for vectors
\mathbf{a,b}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
with

\mathbf{a}=(a_{j})_{j=1}^{3}
,
\mathbf{b=}(b_{j})_{j=1}^{3}
the bilinear
form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
by
\langle\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\rangle:=\sum_{j=1}^{3}a_{j}b_{j}
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we introduce the
seminorms

\left|\mathbf{f}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)}:=\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3}\colon|\alpha|=m}\frac{|\alpha|!}{\alpha!}\left(\partial^{\alpha}\mathbf{f},\partial^{\alpha}\mathbf{f}\right)\right)^{1/2}

(2.2)

and the full norms
\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)}^{2}:=\sum_{n=0}^{m}|\mathbf{f}|_{\mathbf{H}^{n}(\Omega)}^{2}
. For the Maxwell
problem the space
\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{curl})
is
the key to describe the energy of the electric field. For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we set

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{m}\left(\operatorname{curl},\Omega\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)\mid\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)\right\}\quad\text{and}

(2.3a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega):=\mathbf{H}^{0}(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega).

(2.3b)

The
space
\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega)

is given for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
by

\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname{div},\Omega):=\left\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)\mid\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\in H^{m}(\Omega)\right\}

(2.4)

with
\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{div},\Omega):=\mathbf{H}^{0}(\operatorname{div},\Omega)
. We introduce

\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{0},\Omega):=\left\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega)\mid\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}=0\right\}.

(2.5)

For
\rho\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
m
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we define the indexed norms and seminorms by

\left|\mathbf{v}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{\ell}(\Omega),\rho}:=\left|\rho\right|^{-\ell}\left|\mathbf{v}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{\ell}(\Omega)}\quad\text{and\quad}\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega),\rho}:=\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\left|\mathbf{v}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{\ell}(\Omega),\rho}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(2.6)

and corresponding dual norms

\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-m}(\Omega),\rho}:=\left|\rho\right|^{m}\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-m}(\Omega)}.

(2.7)

We define for
\operatorname*{D}\in\left\{\operatorname*{curl},\operatorname*{div}\right\}

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{D},\Omega),\rho}

\displaystyle:=\left(\rho^{-2m}\left|\operatorname*{D}\mathbf{f}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)}^{2}+\rho^{2}\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega),\rho}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\rho^{-2m}\left|\operatorname*{D}\mathbf{f}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}\left(\Omega\right)}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\rho^{2-2\ell}\left|\mathbf{f}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{\ell}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

and introduce the shorthands:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}\left(\operatorname*{D},\Omega\right)}

\displaystyle:=\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}\left(\operatorname*{D},\Omega\right),1},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}\left(\operatorname*{D},\Omega\right),\rho}

\displaystyle:=\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{0}\left(\operatorname*{D},\Omega\right),\rho}=\left(\left\|\operatorname*{D}\mathbf{f}\right\|^{2}+\rho^{2}\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|^{2}\right)^{1/2},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}\left(\operatorname*{D},\Omega\right)}

\displaystyle:=\left\|\mathbf{f}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{0}\left(\operatorname*{D},\Omega\right)}.

(2.9)

We close this section with the introduction of the spaces of analytic functions:

Definition 2.1

For an open set
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, constants

C_{1}
,
\gamma_{1}>0
, and wavenumber
\left|k\right|\geq 1
, we
set

\mathcal{A}(C_{1},\gamma_{1},\omega):=\left\{{\mathbf{u}}\in(C^{\infty}(\omega))^{3}\mid\left|{\mathbf{u}}\right|_{\mathbf{H}^{n}(\omega)}\leq C_{1}\gamma_{1}^{n}\max\left\{n+1,\left|k\right|\right\}^{n}\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}.

2.2
Sobolev spaces on a sufficiently smooth surface
\Gamma

The Sobolev spaces on the boundary
\Gamma
are denoted by
H^{s}(\Gamma)

for scalar-valued functions and by
\mathbf{H}^{s}(\Gamma)
for
vector-valued functions with norms
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}
,
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{s}(\Gamma)}
(see,
e.g.,
[
McL00
, p. 98]
). Note that the range of
s
for which
H^{s}(\Gamma)
is defined may be limited, depending on the global smoothness of the
surface
\Gamma
; for Lipschitz surfaces,
s
can be chosen in the range

\left[0,1\right]
. For
s<0
, the space
H^{s}(\Gamma)
is the dual of

H^{-s}(\Gamma)
.

For a scalar-valued function
u
and vector-valued function
\mathbf{v}
on

\Gamma
, both sufficiently smooth,
the constant extensions (along the normal direction) into a sufficiently
small three-dimensional neighborhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of
\Gamma
is denoted by

u^{\ast}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
. The
surface gradient

\nabla_{\Gamma}
, the
tangential curl

\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}
, and the
surface divergence

\operatorname*{div}_{\Gamma}
are defined by (cf., e.g.,
[
Néd01
]
,

[
BCS02
]
)

\nabla_{\Gamma}u:=\left.\left(\nabla u^{\star}\right)\right|_{\Gamma}\text{,\quad}\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}u:=\nabla_{\Gamma}u\times\mathbf{n}\text{,\quad and\quad}\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}=\left.\left(\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{v}^{\ast}\right)\right|_{\Gamma}\qquad\text{on }\Gamma\text{.}

(2.10)

The scalar counterpart of the tangential curl is the
surface curl

\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}:=\left\langle\left.\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{v}^{\ast}\right)\right|_{\Gamma},\mathbf{n}\right\rangle\qquad\text{on }\Gamma.

(2.11)

The composition of the surface divergence and surface gradient leads to the

scalar Laplace-Beltrami operator
(see
[
Néd01
, (2.5.191)]
)

\Delta_{\Gamma}u=\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}u=-\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}u.

(2.12)

From
[
Néd01
, (2.5.197)]
it follows that

\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\left(\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{n}\right)=\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}.

Next, we introduce Hilbert spaces of tangential fields on the compact and

simply connected
manifold
\Gamma
and corresponding norms and refer
for their definitions and properties to
[
Néd01
, Sec. 5.4.1]
. We start
with the definition of the space
\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)
of tangential
vector fields given by

\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma):=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)\mid\left\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{v}\right\rangle=0\text{ on }\Gamma\right\}.

(2.13)

Any tangential field
\mathbf{v}_{T}
on
\Gamma
then can be represented in
terms of the
Hodge decomposition
2
2

2

Throughout the paper we use
the convention that if
\mathbf{v}_{T}
,
\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla}
,

\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}
,
V^{\nabla}
,

V^{\operatorname*{curl}}
appear in the same context they are related by
(
2.14
).
as

\mathbf{v}_{T}=\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla}+\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\quad\text{with\quad}\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla}:=\nabla_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla}\quad\text{and\quad}\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}:=\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}

(2.14)

for some scalar potentials

V^{\nabla}\in H^{1}(\Gamma)\quad\text{and\quad}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\in H(\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}},\Gamma):=\left\{\phi\in L^{2}(\Gamma)\mid\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}\phi\in\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)\right\}.

In particular, this decomposition is
\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}
-orthogonal:

\left(\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla},\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}=\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla},\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}=0\quad\forall\,\mathbf{v}_{T}=\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla}+\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname{curl}}\text{ as in
(\ref{vFExp}).}

Hence, the splitting (
2.14
) is stable:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\|\nabla_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\left\|\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}\quad\text{and\quad}\left\|\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

Higher order spaces are defined for
s>0
by

\mathbf{H}_{T}^{s}(\Gamma):=\left\{\mathbf{v}_{T}\in\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)\mid\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{s}(\Gamma)}<\infty\right\}

(2.15)

and for negative
s
by duality.

The
H^{s}(\Gamma)
-norm of
\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\left(\cdot\right)
and
\operatorname*{div}_{\Gamma}\left(\cdot\right)
can
be expressed by using the Hodge decomposition:

\displaystyle\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle=\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\!=\!\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\!\!=\!\left\|\Delta_{\Gamma}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)},

\displaystyle\left\|\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle=\left\|\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}=\left\|\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}=\left\|\Delta_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla}\right\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}.

We define

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)}

\displaystyle:=\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\|\Delta_{\Gamma}V^{\operatorname*{curl}}\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

(2.16a)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\operatorname{div}_{\Gamma},\Gamma)}

\displaystyle:=\left(\left\|\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T}^{\nabla}\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\|\Delta_{\Gamma}V^{\nabla}\right\|_{H^{-1/2}\left(\Gamma\right)}^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(2.16b)

The corresponding spaces
\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)
are characterized by

\begin{array}[c]{ccc}\mathbf{v}_{T}\in\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)&\iff&\mathbf{v}_{T}\text{ has form (\ref{vFExp}) and
}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}\left(\operatorname{div}_{\Gamma},\Gamma\right)}<\infty,\\
\mathbf{v}_{T}\in\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)&\iff&\mathbf{v}_{T}\text{ has form (\ref{vFExp}) and
}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma\right)}<\infty.\end{array}

(2.17)

We also introduce indexed norms for functions in Sobolev spaces on the
boundary: for
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
with
2\nu\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we formally set

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\nu}(\Gamma),k}

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{2\nu}\left|k\right|^{1-\ell}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\ell/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\quad\text{and}

(2.18a)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-\nu}(\Gamma),k}

\displaystyle:=\left|k\right|^{\nu+1/2}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-\nu}(\Gamma)}.

(2.18b)

For
\operatorname*{D}_{\Gamma}\in\left\{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\operatorname*{div}_{\Gamma}\right\}
, we
introduce
3
3

3

We always write
\left|k\right|
in the estimates also if the
exponent is even for the sake of clarity.

\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\nu}(\operatorname*{D}_{\Gamma},\Gamma),k}:=\left(\left\|\operatorname*{D}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\nu}(\Gamma),k}^{2}+\left|k\right|^{2}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\nu}(\Gamma),k}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(2.19)

In particular, we have

\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{0}(\Gamma),k}=\left|k\right|^{1/2}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\quad\text{and\quad}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\nu}\left(\Gamma\right)}\leq C\left|k\right|^{-1/2+\nu}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{\nu}\left(\Gamma\right),k}.

We remark that the special dual norms
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\operatorname{div}_{\Gamma},\Gamma),k}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}^{\prime}(\Gamma),k}
on the
boundary will be defined later in (
4.4
) and in
(
4.5
). By using standard interpolation inequalities for Sobolev
spaces we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2

For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there holds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m+1/2}(\Gamma),k}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|k\right|\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\sum\limits_{r=1}^{m+1}\left|k\right|^{2-2r}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\displaystyle\sum\limits_{r=0}^{m+1}\left|k\right|^{2-2r}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{r-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m+1/2}(\Gamma),k}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|k\right|\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\left|k\right|^{-2m}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m+1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|k\right|^{2}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}+\left|k\right|^{-2m}\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m+1/2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

2.3
Trace operators and energy spaces for Maxwell’s equations

We introduce tangential trace operators
\Pi_{T}
and
\gamma_{T}
, which map
sufficiently smooth functions
\mathbf{u}
in
\overline{\Omega}
to
tangential fields on
\Gamma
, by

\Pi_{T}:\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{n}\times\left(\mathbf{u}|_{\Gamma}\times\mathbf{n}\right),\quad\gamma_{T}:\mathbf{u}\mapsto\mathbf{u}|_{\Gamma}\times\mathbf{n}.

(2.21)

The following theorem shows that
\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)
are the correct spaces for the continuous
extension of the tangential trace operators to Hilbert spaces.

Proposition 2.3
(
[
Ces96
]
,
[
Néd01
, Thm. 5.4.2]
)

The
trace mappings
\Pi_{T}
and
\gamma_{T}
in (
2.21
) extend
to continuous and surjective operators

\Pi_{T}:\mathbf{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma),\qquad\gamma_{T}:\mathbf{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma).

Moreover, for theses trace spaces there exist continuous divergence-free
liftings
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{curl}}^{\Gamma}:\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbf{X}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{div}}^{\Gamma}:\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbf{X}
.

For a vector field
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}
, we will employ frequently the
notation

\mathbf{u}_{T}:=\Pi_{T}\mathbf{u}.

From
[
Néd01
, (2.5.161), (2.5.208)]
and the relation
\Pi_{T}\nabla u\mathbf{=n\times}\left(\left.\nabla u\right|_{\Gamma}\times\mathbf{n}\right)=\left.\nabla u\right|_{\Gamma}-\left(\partial_{\mathbf{n}}u\right)\mathbf{n}
we conclude

\displaystyle\Pi_{T}\nabla u

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\Gamma}(u|_{\Gamma}),

(2.22)

\displaystyle\gamma_{T}\nabla u

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{T}\nabla u)\times\mathbf{n}=\nabla_{\Gamma}(u|_{\Gamma})\times\mathbf{n}.

(2.23)

Remark 2.4

For gradient fields
\nabla\varphi
we
have
(\nabla\varphi)_{T}^{\operatorname*{curl}}=0
and
(\nabla\varphi)_{T}^{\nabla}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi
.

Definition 2.5

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded domain with
sufficiently smooth Lipschitz boundary
\Gamma
as described in
Section
2.1
. The
energy space
for Maxwell’s equations with

impedance boundary conditions
on
\Gamma

and
real wavenumber
k\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left(-k_{0},k_{0}\right)
is

\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}:=\left\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}\colon\Pi_{T}\mathbf{u\in L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)\right\}

(2.24)

with corresponding norm

\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\operatorname*{imp},k}:=\left[\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{k,+}^{2}\right]^{1/2}\text{ with }\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{k,+}:=\left[k^{2}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{2}+\left|k\right|\left\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\right]^{1/2}.

Its
companion space
of scalar potentials is

H_{\operatorname*{imp}}^{1}(\Omega):=\left\{\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega)\mid\left.\varphi\right|_{\Gamma}\in H^{1}(\Gamma)\right\}.

(2.25)

2.4
Regular decompositions

We will rely on various decompositions of functions into regular parts and
gradient parts.
The decompositions may not be orthogonal but must be stable. We refer to

[
Hip02
, §4.4]
and the bibliographic notes therein for some
early contributions. Many variants have been introduced since then, and the
results in this section are essentially taken from the literature: Lemma
2.6

is a consequence of
[
CM10
, Thm. 4.6]
;
Lemma
2.7
relies on
[
Mon03
, Thm. 3.38]

and Lemma
2.6
; Lemma
2.8
is based on

[
Cos90
]
while closely related results can be found in

[
ABDG98
]
. Finally,
Lemma
2.9
is a consequence of
[
Sch18
, Thm. 4.2(2)]
and
[
Cos90
]
. For newer overview
articles, we refer to, e.g.,
[
Hip15
]
,
[
HP19
]
.The following Lemma
2.6
collects a key result from the seminal paper

[
CM10
]
. The operator
\mathbf{R}_{2}
, which is
essentially a right inverse of the curl operator, will frequently be employed
in the present paper.

Lemma 2.6

Let
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. There exist
pseudodifferential operators
R_{1}
,

\mathbf{R}_{2}

of order
-1
and
\mathbf{K}
,
\mathbf{K}_{2}
of order
-\infty
on

{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with the following properties: For each
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}

they have the mapping properties
R_{1}:\mathbf{H}^{-m}(\Omega)\rightarrow H^{1-m}(\Omega)
,
\mathbf{R}_{2}:\mathbf{H}^{-m}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{1-m}(\Omega)
, and
\mathbf{K}
,

\mathbf{K}_{2}:\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)\rightarrow({C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}))^{3}
, and for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega)
there holds

\mathbf{u}=\nabla R_{1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}))+\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})+\mathbf{Ku}.

(2.26)

For
\mathbf{u}
with
\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}=0
on
\Omega
there
holds

\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{K}_{2}\mathbf{u}.

(2.27)

Proof.
In
[
CM10
, Thm. 4.6]
, operators
R_{1}
,
\mathbf{R}_{2}
,
\mathbf{R}_{3}
,
\mathbf{K}_{1}
,
\mathbf{K}_{2}
with the mapping
properties

\begin{array}[c]{cl}R_{1}:\mathbf{H}^{-m}(\Omega)\rightarrow H^{1-m}\left(\Omega\right),&\\
\mathbf{R}_{2}:\mathbf{H}^{-m}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{1-m}\left(\Omega\right),&\\
\mathbf{R}_{3}:H^{-m}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{1-m}\left(\Omega\right),&\\
\mathbf{K}_{\ell}:\mathbf{H}^{m}\left(\Omega\right)\rightarrow({C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega}))^{3},&\ell=1,2,\end{array}

(2.28)

are constructed with

\displaystyle\nabla R_{1}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{K}_{1}\mathbf{v},

(2.29a)

\displaystyle\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{R}_{3}\left(\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{v}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{K}_{2}\mathbf{v}.

(2.29b)

We note that (
2.29
) implies (
2.27
). It is worth stressing
that the mapping properties given in (
2.28
) express a locality of
the operators, which are pseudodifferential operators on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
: on

\Omega
, the operators depend only on the argument restricted to
\Omega
and
not on the values on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
.

Selecting
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})
in (
2.29a
) we obtain

\begin{split}\nabla R_{1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}))+\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})))=\\
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})-\mathbf{K}_{1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})).\end{split}

(2.30)

Since
\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}
is divergence free, we obtain from
(
2.29b
)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}))\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right)-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{K}_{2}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{2}(\mathbf{K}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}))=:\mathbf{K}_{3}\mathbf{u},

where, again,
\mathbf{K}_{3}
is a smoothing operator of order
-\infty
.
Inserting this into (
2.30
) leads to

\nabla R_{1}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right)\right)+\mathbf{R}_{2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right)=\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{K}_{1}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right)\right)-\mathbf{K}_{3}\mathbf{u}.

By choosing
\mathbf{K}\mathbf{u}:=\mathbf{K}_{1}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right)+\mathbf{K}_{3}\mathbf{u}
the representation (
2.26
) is proved.

Lemma 2.7

Let
\Omega
be a bounded, connected
Lipschitz domain.

(i)

There is
C>0
such that
for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}
there is a decomposition
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\mathbf{z}
with

\operatorname{div}\mathbf{z}=0,\quad\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|,\quad\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}\left(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega\right)}.

(2.31)

(ii)

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. For
each
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega)

there is a splitting independent of
m
of the form
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\mathbf{z}
with
\varphi\in H^{m+1}(\Omega)
,

\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{H}^{m+1}(\Omega)
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m+1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega)}\quad\text{and\hskip 10.22217pt}

(2.32a)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{m+1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{m}(\Omega)}.

(2.32b)

(iii)

There is
C>0

depending only on
\Omega
such that each
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname{imp}}
can be written as
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\mathbf{z}
with
\varphi\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{imp}}(\Omega)
,
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
and

\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}+\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}+|k|\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+|k|^{1/2}\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}.

(2.33)

Proof.

Proof of (
i
):
Let

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}
. The point is to choose
\mathbf{z}
in the
splitting
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\mathbf{z}
such that it can be controlled
by
\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}
. To this end, we set
\mathbf{v}=\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)
and observe that

\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{v}=0
and therefore
(1,\langle{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}=0
. By
[
Mon03
, Thm. 3.38]
,
this allows us to conclude the existence of
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
with
\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{z}=0
,
\mathbf{v}=\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}
and

\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|.

Since
\operatorname*{curl}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z})=0
, we have
\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}=\nabla\varphi
for a
\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, which trivially
satisfies

\left(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\psi\right)=\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z},\nabla\psi\right)\qquad\forall\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega).

By fixing
\varphi
such that
\int_{\Omega}\varphi=0
, the estimate of

\varphi
follows by a Poincaré inequality.

Proof of (
ii
):
With the
operators of Lemma
2.6
, we define

\mathbf{z}:=\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u})+\mathbf{K}\mathbf{u},\qquad\nabla\varphi:=\nabla R_{1}(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{u})).

Lemma
2.6
implies
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{z}+\nabla\varphi
as well as the
bounds by the mapping properties given in Lemma
2.6
.

Proof of (
iii
):
Multiplying
estimate (
2.32b
) for the
decomposition of (
ii
) and
m=0
by

\left|k\right|
leads to
\left|k\right|\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left|k\right|\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left|k\right|\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}
. (
2.32a
) gives

\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega)}
.
The multiplicative trace inequality gives

|k|\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}\leq C\left|k\right|\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left|k\right|^{2}\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+C\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}
. Hence follows
\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\leq C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega),k}\leq C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}
. The triangle inequality then provides the bound
\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\leq\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}+\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\leq C\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}
.

The following result relates the space
\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega)\cap\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{div},\Omega)
to classical Sobolev
spaces. The statement (
2.35
) is from
[
Cos90
]
;
closely related results can be found in

[
ABDG98
]
.

Lemma 2.8

Let
\partial\Omega
be smooth and simply
connected. Then there is
C>0
such that for every
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}\left(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega\right)\cap\mathbf{H}\left(\operatorname{div},\Omega\right)
there holds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\left\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{n}\right\rangle\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}\right),

(2.34a)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}\right).

(2.34b)

Under the assumption
4
4

4

In
[
Cos90
]
, it is shown that these
conditions are equivalent for
{\mathbf{u}}
with
{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbf{H}}(\Omega,\operatorname{curl})\cap H(\Omega,\operatorname{div})
.
that

\left\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{n}\right\rangle\in L^{2}(\Gamma)
or
\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u\in L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)
, there holds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\left\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{n}\right\rangle\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}\right),

(2.35a)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}\right).

(2.35b)

Proof.
We use the regular decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\mathbf{z},
of Lemma
2.7
(
i
)
where
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
satisfies

\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|.

(2.36)

Since
\operatorname{div}\mathbf{z}=0
we have
\displaystyle\Delta\varphi=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}.
Concerning the boundary conditions for

\varphi
, we consider two cases corresponding to (
2.34a
),
(
2.35a
) and (
2.34b
),
(
2.35b
) separately as Case 1 and Case 2.

Case 1.
The function
\varphi
satisfies the Neumann problem

\Delta\varphi=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u},\qquad\partial_{n}\varphi=\left\langle\mathbf{n},\nabla\varphi\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\right\rangle,

and we note that the condition
\operatorname{div}\mathbf{z}=0
implies that
the solvability condition for this Neumann problem is satisfied. We estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\left\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\right\rangle\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\left\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}+\left\|\left\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{z}\right\rangle\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\left\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\|_{H^{-1/2}\left(\Gamma\right)}+C\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}.

An energy estimate for
\varphi
provides

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|\leq C(\left\|\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\partial_{n}\varphi\right\|_{H^{-1/2}\left(\Gamma\right)}).

The combination of these estimates lead to (
2.34a
). We also
note that if
\left\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{n}\right\rangle\in L^{2}(\Gamma)
, then we get by the smoothness of
\Gamma
that
\varphi\in H^{3/2}(\Omega)
with
\|\varphi\|_{H^{3/2}(\Omega)}\leq C(\|\operatorname{div}{\mathbf{u}}\|+\|\partial_{n}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)})
, which shows (
2.35a
).

Case 2.
We obtain regularity assertions for
\varphi
by using that
\varphi
satisfies

\Delta\varphi=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}
and determine the boundary regularity
\varphi|_{\Gamma}
.
We observe

\overrightarrow{\operatorname*{curl}\nolimits_{\Gamma}}\varphi=\gamma_{T}\nabla\varphi=\gamma_{T}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\right)

and therefore

\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi=-\operatorname{curl}_{\Gamma}\overrightarrow{\operatorname{curl}_{\Gamma}}\varphi=-\operatorname{curl}_{\Gamma}\left(\gamma_{T}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\right)\right).

Hence, by smoothness of
\partial\Omega
(and the fact that
\partial\Omega

is connected) we get

\displaystyle\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi\right\|_{H^{-3/2}(\Gamma)}=C\left\|\operatorname{curl}_{\Gamma}\left(\gamma_{T}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{-3/2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\gamma_{T}\left(\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{z}\right)\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}\leq C\left(\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Gamma)}+\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\right).

Since
\|\varphi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C(\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\|+\|\varphi\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma)})
we get
(
2.34b
). By similar reasoning,
\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)
implies
\varphi|_{\Gamma}\in H^{1}(\Gamma)
with
\|\varphi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq C(\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)})
so that
\varphi\in H^{3/2}(\Omega)
and thus (
2.35b
).

The following lemma introduces some variants of Helmholtz decompositions.

Lemma 2.9

Let
\Omega
be a bounded sufficiently smooth
Lipschitz domain with simply connected boundary. For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\cap\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega)
, there exist
\varphi\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\cap H^{3/2}(\Omega)

and
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
with

\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{z}=0
such that
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}
. The function
\mathbf{u}
belongs to

\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)
, and we have the estimates

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)},

(2.37a)

\displaystyle\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right).

(2.37b)

If
\mathbf{u}
admits a decomposition of the form
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{r}+\operatorname*{curl}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
with
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)
, then
the decomposition
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\varphi+\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{z}
satisfies

\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|\mathbf{r}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}.

(2.37c)

Proof.
The Helmholtz decomposition was considered in
[
Sch18
, Thm. 4.2(2)]
,
[
Sch09
, Thm. 28(i)]
. Since

\operatorname*{div}\operatorname*{curl}=0
and we require
\varphi\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
, we have

\Delta\varphi=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}\quad\text{and\quad}\varphi|_{\partial\Omega}=0.

(2.38)

Lemma
2.8
implies for

\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\cap\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega)

that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)
. A standard shift theorem for the
Poisson equation leads to

\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{3/2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|\operatorname{div}{\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{H^{-1/2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\|{\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}.

(2.39)

Next, we estimate
\mathbf{z}
. Note that
\varphi\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)

implies
\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi=0
so that also
\gamma_{T}\nabla\varphi=0
on

\Gamma
. Lemma
2.8
then implies

\displaystyle\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\left\|\gamma_{T}\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\gamma_{T}\nabla\varphi=0}{=}C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right).

The estimate (
2.37c
) follows from (
2.39
) via

\operatorname{div}\mathbf{u}=\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{r}
.
This finishes the proof of (
2.37
).

2.5
Maxwell’s equations with impedance boundary
conditions

We have introduced all basic ingredients to formulate the electric Maxwell
equations for constant wavenumber
k\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left(-k_{0},k_{0}\right)
with impedance boundary conditions on
\Gamma
. We define the
sesquilinear form
A_{k}:\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\times\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}):=\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u,}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{v}\right)-k^{2}\left(\mathbf{u,v}\right)-\operatorname*{i}k\left(\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{v}_{T}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(2.40)

The variational formulation is: Given an electric current density
\mathbf{j}

and boundary data
\mathbf{g}_{T}
with

\mathbf{j}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega),\qquad\mathbf{g}_{T}\in\mathbf{H}_{T}^{-1/2}\left(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma},\Gamma\right)\cap\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)

(2.41)

find
\mathbf{E}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}
such that

A_{k}(\mathbf{E},\mathbf{v})=\left(\mathbf{j,v}\right)+\left(\mathbf{g}_{T},\mathbf{v}_{T}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\quad\forall\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}.

(2.42)

Note that the assumptions (
2.41
) on the data are not the
most general ones (see (
4.1
), (
4.2
) and
(
4.17
) below) but they reduce
technicalities in some places. By integration by parts it is easy to see that
the classical strong form of this equation is given by

\begin{array}[c]{ll}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega,k}\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{j}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{g}_{T}&\text{on }\Gamma\end{array}

(2.43)

with the volume and boundary differential operators
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega,k}

and
\mathcal{B}_{\Omega,k}
, defined by

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega,k}\mathbf{v}:=\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{v}-k^{2}\mathbf{v}\text{ in }\Omega\quad\text{and\quad}\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\mathbf{v}:=\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{v}-\operatorname*{i}k\Pi_{T}\mathbf{v}\text{ on }\Gamma\mathbf{.}

We denote by

\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}:\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname{imp}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname{imp}}

(2.44)

the solution operator that maps the linear functional
\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\ni\mathbf{v}\rightarrow\left(\mathbf{j,v}\right)+\left(\mathbf{g}_{T},\mathbf{v}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}
to the solution
\mathbf{E}
of (
2.43
) and whose
existence follows from Proposition
3.1
below.

In our analysis, the sesquilinear form

{\left(\!\left(\kern-0.50003pt\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\kern-1.00006pt\right)\!\right)}_{k}:=k^{2}\left(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\right)+\operatorname*{i}k\left(\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{v}_{T}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}

(2.45)

will play an important role. We note

\displaystyle A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u,}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{v}\right)-{\left(\!\left(\kern-0.50003pt\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\kern-1.00006pt\right)\!\right)}_{k},

(2.46)

\displaystyle A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\nabla\varphi)

\displaystyle=-{\left(\!\left(\kern-0.50003pt\mathbf{u},\nabla\varphi\kern-1.00006pt\right)\!\right)}_{k}\qquad\forall\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}},\quad\forall\varphi\in H_{\operatorname*{imp}}^{1}(\Omega),

(2.47)

\displaystyle{\left(\!\left(\kern-0.50003pt\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\kern-1.00006pt\right)\!\right)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\overline{{\left(\!\left(\kern-0.50003pt\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\kern-1.00006pt\right)\!\right)}_{k}}.

(2.48)

3
Stability analysis of the continuous Maxwell problem

In this section we show that the model problem (
2.42
) is
well-posed and that the norm of the solution operator is
O(|k|^{\theta})
for
suitable choices of norms and some
\theta\geq 0
.

3.1
Well-posedness

The continuity of the sesquilinear form
A_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is obvious: it
holds

\left|A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\right|\leq C_{\operatorname*{cont}}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\operatorname*{imp},k}\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|_{\operatorname*{imp},k}\qquad\text{with\qquad}C_{\operatorname*{cont}}:=1\text{.}

Well-posedness of the Maxwell problem with impedance condition is proved in

[
Mon03
, Thm. 4.17]
. Here we recall the statement and give a sketch
of the proof.

Proposition 3.1

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a
bounded Lipschitz domain with simply connected and sufficiently smooth
boundary. Then there exists
\gamma_{k}>0
such that

\gamma_{k}\leq\inf_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\backslash\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}}\sup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}\backslash\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}}\frac{\left|A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\right|}{\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|_{\operatorname*{imp},k}\left\|\mathbf{v}\right\|_{\operatorname*{imp},k}}.

Proof.

Step 1.
We show uniqueness. If
A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=0
for
all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}
then

0=\operatorname{Im}A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u})=-k\left\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}^{2}.

Hence,
\mathbf{u}_{T}=\mathbf{0}
on
\Gamma
and the extension of

\mathbf{u}
by zero outside of
\Omega
(denoted
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
) is
in
\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\widetilde{\Omega})
for any bounded domain

\widetilde{\Omega}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This zero extension
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
solves the homogeneous Maxwell equations on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. An
application of the operator “
\operatorname{div}
” shows that
\operatorname{div}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}=0

and thus
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Using

\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{curl}=-\Delta+\nabla\operatorname{div}
we
see that each component of
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
solves the homogeneous
Helmholtz equation. Since
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
vanishes outside
\Omega
,
the unique continuation principle asserts
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}=0
.

Step 2.
From
[
GM12
, Thm. 4.8]
or the technique developed in
[
Buf05
]

it follows that the operator induced by
A_{k}
is a compact perturbation of
an isomorphism and the Fredholm alternative shows well-posedness of the problem.

3.2
Wavenumber-explicit stability estimates

Proposition
3.1
does not give any insight how
the (positive) inf-sup constant
\gamma_{k}
depends on the wavenumber
k
. In
this section, we introduce the stability constant
C_{\operatorname*{stab}}(k)

and estimate its dependence on
k
under certain assumptions.

Definition 3.2

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded Lipschitz
domain with simply connected and sufficiently smooth boundary.
Let
C_{\operatorname*{stab}}(k)
denote the smallest constant such that
for each
\mathbf{j}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{g}_{T}\in\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)
the solution
\mathbf{E}=\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}({\mathbf{j}},{\mathbf{g}}_{T})
of
(
2.42
) satisfies

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{E}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\leq C_{\operatorname*{stab}}\left(k\right)\left(\left\|\mathbf{j}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right).

(3.1)

The behavior of the constant

C_{\operatorname*{stab}}\left(k\right)

with respect to the wavenumber typically depends on the geometry of the domain

\Omega
. Our stability and convergence theory for conforming Galerkin finite
element discretization as presented in Sections
9
requires
that this constant grow at most algebraically in
k
, i.e.,

\exists\theta\geq 0,C_{\operatorname{stab}}>0\quad\text{such that }C_{\operatorname*{stab}}\left(k\right)\leq C_{\operatorname{stab}}|k|^{\theta}\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left(-k_{0},k_{0}\right).

(3.2)

Remark 3.3

For the

hp
-FEM application below, the term
\left|k\right|^{\theta}
will
be mitigated by an exponentially converging approximation term so that
any finite value
\theta\geq 0
leads to an exponential convergence of the
discretization.

Next we present an example
5
5

5

We thank an anonymous referee for suggesting
this example.
which shows that in general the exponent
\theta
in
(
3.2
) cannot be negative.

Example 3.4

Let
\Omega=\left(-2,2\right)^{3}
,
\omega:=\left(-1,1\right)^{2}
. Define the cutoff function
\chi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\chi\left(\mathbf{x}\right):=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{ll}\left(1-x_{1}^{2}\right)^{2}\left(1-x_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}\left(1-x_{3}^{2}\right)^{2}&\mathbf{x}=\left(x_{j}\right)_{j=1}^{3}\in\omega,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Note that
\chi\in H_{0}^{2}\left(\Omega\right)
. With

\mathbf{e}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
1\leq j\leq 3
, denoting the
j
-th canonical unit vector
we define

\mathbf{j}:=\operatorname*{e}\nolimits^{\operatorname*{i}kx_{1}}\left(-\left(\Delta\chi+\operatorname*{i}k\partial_{1}\chi\right)\mathbf{e}_{2}+\nabla\partial_{2}\chi+\operatorname*{i}k\operatorname*{curl}\left(\chi\mathbf{e}_{3}\right)\right).

Then,
\mathbf{E:}=\chi\operatorname*{e}^{\operatorname*{i}kx_{1}}\mathbf{e}_{2}
is the unique weak solution of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Omega,k}\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{j}

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\text{ on }\Gamma.

Using the symbolic computer algebra program
MATHEMATICA
we obtain explicitly

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{j}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Omega\right)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{16777216\left(5k^{2}+33\right)}{10418625},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{E}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{curl},\Omega),k}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\frac{16777216\left(k^{2}+3\right)}{31255875},\quad\left\|\mathbf{E}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}=0,

which shows that in general
\theta\geq 0
in (
3.1
).

Remark 3.5

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain with simply
connected and sufficiently smooth boundary. The sesquilinear form
A_{k}

satisfies the inf-sup condition

\inf_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega)}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega)}\frac{|A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})|}{\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}}\geq\frac{1}{1+|k|C_{\operatorname{stab}}\left(k\right)}.

(3.3)

This result is shown in the same way as in the Helmholtz case, see, e.g.,

[
EM12
, Thm. 2.5]
,
[
Mel95
, Prop. 8.2.7]
.
If assumption (
3.2
) holds, then

\inf_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega)}\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{curl},\Omega)}\frac{|A_{k}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})|}{\|\mathbf{u}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}}\geq\frac{1}{1+C_{\operatorname{stab}}|k|^{\theta+1}}.

In the remaining part of this section, we prove estimate
(
3.2
) for certain classes of domains. The following
result removes the assumption in
[
HMP11
]
for the
right-hand side to be solenoidal.

Proposition 3.6

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded

C^{2}
domain that is star-shaped with respect to a ball. Then, assumption
(
3.2
) holds with
\theta=0
.

Proof.
Let
\varphi\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
satisfy

-\Delta\varphi=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{j}\qquad\mbox{ in $\Omega$}\text{.}

Then,

\displaystyle\|k^{-2}\nabla\varphi\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}

\displaystyle=|k|^{-1}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C|k|^{-1}\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{j}\|_{H^{-1}(\Omega)}\leq C|k|^{-1}\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{j}+\nabla\varphi\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+C\|\operatorname{div}\mathbf{j}\|_{H^{-1}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

Noting that
\varphi
vanishes on
\Gamma
, the difference
\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})-\left|k\right|^{-2}\nabla\varphi
satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega,k}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})-\left|k\right|^{-2}\nabla\varphi\right)=\mathbf{j}+\nabla\varphi,\quad\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\!\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})-\left|k\right|^{-2}\nabla\varphi\right)=\mathbf{g}_{T},

and
\operatorname{div}(\mathbf{j}+\nabla\varphi)=0
.
[
HMP11
, Thm. 3.1]
implies

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})-\left|k\right|^{-2}\nabla\varphi\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\mathbf{j}+\nabla\varphi\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|\mathbf{j}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right).

The estimate for
\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})
follows from a triangle inequality.

For the more general situation of domains that are not necessarily star-shaped we
require some preliminaries. A bounded domain
\Omega
with smooth boundary
admits, e.g., by
[
HLZ12
, Cor. 4.1]
a
continuous extension operator
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{div}}:\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{m}(\operatorname*{div},\mathbb{R}^{3})
for any

m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In particular this extension can be chosen such that for
a ball
B_{R}
of radius
R
with
\Omega\subset B_{R}
there holds

\operatorname*{supp}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{div}}\mathbf{h}\right)\subset B_{R}\quad\forall\,\mathbf{h}\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega).

(3.4)

Since the right-hand side
\mathbf{j}
in (
2.43
), in general, does not
belong to
\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega)
we subtract an appropriate
gradient field: Let
\psi\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
be the weak solution of

-\Delta\psi=\operatorname{div}\mathbf{j}
. As in the proof of
Proposition
3.6
, we write
\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})=\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{j}},\mathbf{g}_{T})-k^{-2}\nabla\psi
with

\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}:=\mathbf{j}+\nabla\psi\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\Omega).

(3.5)

The operator
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{div}}
allows us to extend

\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}
to a compactly supported function
\mathbf{J}:=\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{div}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{j}})\in\mathbf{H}(\operatorname*{div},\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Next we introduce the solution operator for the full space problem

\begin{array}[c]{cl}\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{Z}-k^{2}\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{J}&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\left|\partial_{r}\mathbf{Z}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)-\operatorname*{i}k\mathbf{Z}\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right|\leq c/r^{2}&\text{as
}r=\left\|\mathbf{x}\right\|\rightarrow\infty,\end{array}

(3.6)

via the Maxwell potential

\mathbf{Z}=\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}\left(\mathbf{J}\right):=\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}\left(\mathbf{J}\right)+\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left(\mathbf{J}\right),

(3.7a)

where
6
6

6

With a slight abuse of notation we write
\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left(v\right):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g_{k}\left(\left\|\mathbf{\cdot}-\mathbf{y}\right\|\right)v\left(\mathbf{y}\right)d\mathbf{y}
also for scalar functions

v
. This is the classical acoustic Newton potential.

\left.\begin{array}[c]{l}\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left(\mathbf{J}\right):={\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}}g_{k}\left(\left\|\mathbf{\cdot}-\mathbf{y}\right\|\right)\mathbf{J}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)d\mathbf{y}\\
\\
\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}\left(\mathbf{J}\right):=k^{-2}\nabla\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left(\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{J}\right)\end{array}\right\}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3}

(3.7b)

with the fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

g_{k}\left(r\right):=\frac{\operatorname*{e}\nolimits^{\operatorname*{i}kr}}{4\pi r}\text{.}

(3.7c)

Note that the adjoint full space problem is given by replacing
k
in
(
3.6
) by
-k
with solution operator
\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},-k}\left(\mathbf{J}\right)
.

The layer operators
\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}
,

\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}
map densities defined on
the boundary
\Gamma
to functions defined in
\Omega
by

\left.\begin{array}[c]{l}\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right):={\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}}g_{k}\left(\left\|\mathbf{\cdot}-\mathbf{y}\right\|\right){\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left(\mathbf{y}\right)d\Gamma_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\\
\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}\left({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right):=k^{-2}\nabla\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left(\operatorname*{div}_{\Gamma}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\end{array}\right\}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\Gamma.

(3.8)

We set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{R}^{3},k}^{\operatorname*{MW}}:=\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}+\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}
.

Theorem 3.7

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded
Lipschitz domain with simply connected, analytic boundary. Then, there is
C>0

depending only on
\Omega
such that

C_{\operatorname*{stab}}\left(k\right)\leq C\left|k\right|^{7/2}\sqrt{1+\ln\left|k\right|}.

Remark 3.8

The analyticity requirement of
\partial\Omega
can be relaxed. It is due to
our citing
[
Mel12
]
, which assumes analyticity.

Proof.
We estimate
\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})
(see (
2.44
)) for given
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)\times\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)
.

Step 1.

(reduction to solenoidal right-hand side)

Let
\psi
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}
be as in (
3.5
) so that

\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{g}_{T})=\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{j}},\mathbf{g}_{T})-k^{-2}\nabla\psi
and
\operatorname*{div}\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}=0
.
As in the proof of Proposition
3.6
, we have

\|k^{-2}\nabla\psi\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}\leq C\left|k\right|^{-1}\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)},\quad\|\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)},\quad\operatorname{div}\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}=0.

(3.9)

In particular,

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\|_{\mathbf{H}(\operatorname{div},\Omega)}\leq C\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

Step 2.

(reduction to homogeneous volume right-hand side)
We set

\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}:=\mathbf{g}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}\quad\text{with\quad}\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}:=\left.\left(\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname*{div}}\mathbf{\tilde{j}}\right)\right|_{\Omega}

(3.10)

so that
\mathbf{\tilde{E}}:=\mathcal{S}_{\Omega,k}^{\operatorname{MW}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{j}},\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T})=\mathbf{u}_{0}+\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}
with
\mathbf{u}_{0}
being the
solution of the homogeneous problem

\begin{array}[c]{ll}\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}-k^{2}\mathbf{u}_{0}=\mathbf{0}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\gamma_{T}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)-\operatorname*{i}k\left(\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)_{T}=\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}&\text{on }\Gamma.\end{array}

(3.11)

To estimate
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}
, we rely on the following estimate from

[
MS10
, Lem. 3.5]

|k|\|\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname{MW},k}^{\operatorname{curl}}(f)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname{MW},k}^{\operatorname{curl}}(f)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+|k|^{-1}\|\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname{MW},k}^{\operatorname{curl}}(f)\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}

(3.12)

for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Abbreviate
\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}:=\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname{MW},k}^{\operatorname{curl}}(\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{div}}\widetilde{\mathbf{j}})
and
N^{\nabla}:=\mathcal{N}_{\operatorname{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}(\operatorname{div}\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{div}}\widetilde{\mathbf{j}})
. Estimates (
3.12
)
and (
3.9
)
imply

\displaystyle|k|\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}+|k|^{-1}\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle|k|\|N^{\nabla}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|N^{\nabla}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+|k|^{-1}\|N^{\nabla}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\operatorname{div}\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{div}}\mathbf{\tilde{j}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

For
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}+k^{-2}\nabla N^{\nabla}
we get by a multiplicative trace inequality:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}\|_{\operatorname{imp},k}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(|k|\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}+|k|^{1/2}\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{1/2}\|\mathbf{N}^{\operatorname{curl}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{}+|k|^{-1}\|{N}^{\nabla}\|_{{H}^{1}(\Omega)}+|k|^{-3/2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}{N}^{\nabla}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+|k|^{-1}\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+|k|^{-3/2}\|N^{\nabla}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{1/2}\|N^{\nabla}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.13)

Arguing similarly, we get for
\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}_{T}=\mathbf{g}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathbf{g}}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\mathcal{B}_{\Gamma,k}\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{j}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\mathbf{g}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+|k|^{1/2}\|\mathbf{j}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

(3.14)

Step 3
.
(Estimate of

\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}
,
\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}
.)
To estimate the
function
\mathbf{u}_{0}
, we employ the Stratton-Chu formula (see, e.g.,

[
CK92
, Thm. 6.2]
,
[
Néd01
, (5.5.3)-(5.5.6)]
)

\mathbf{u}_{0}=\operatorname*{curl}\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}\left(\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)+\mathcal{S}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3},k}^{\operatorname*{MW}}\left(\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)\quad\text{in }\Omega.

The weak formulation (
2.42
) implies

\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|^{2}-k^{2}\left\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|^{2}-\operatorname*{i}k\left\|\left(\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}=\left(\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T},\left(\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)_{T}\right)_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}

from which we obtain by considering the imaginary part

\left|k\right|\left\|\left(\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}\leq\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.15)

From the real part, we then obtain by a Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\left|\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\|^{2}-\left|k\right|^{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\|^{2}\right|\leq C\left|k\right|^{-1}\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}^{2}.

(3.16)

Next, we estimate the traces
\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}

and
\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}
. Since
\Pi_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}\in\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)
we may employ
\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}=\left(\Pi_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)\times\mathbf{n}
and (
3.15
) to obtain

\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}=\left\|\Pi_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\frac{1}{\left|k\right|}\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.17)

The boundary conditions (second equation in (
3.11
)) lead to

\left\|\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}+\left|k\right|\left\|\left(\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq 2\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.18)

The estimate (
3.18
) also implies

\left\|\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\|\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq 2\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.19)

Step 4. (Mapping properties of Maxwell Layer Potentials.)

The mapping properties of
\operatorname*{curl}\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}

are well understood due to their relation with the acoustic single layer
potential. We conclude from
[
Mel12
, Lem. 3.4, Thm. 5.3]
:

\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{s}(\Omega)}\leq C_{s}\left|k\right|^{s+1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{s-3/2}(\Gamma)}\quad\text{for }s\geq 0.

Hence, the Stratton-Chu formula leads to the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Omega)}

(3.20)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}(\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}(\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\nabla}(\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}(\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}(\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|+\left|k\right|^{-2}\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\operatorname*{MW},k}^{\operatorname*{curl}}(\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left|k\right|^{3/2}\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+C\left|k\right|\left\|\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-3/2}(\Gamma)}+\left|k\right|^{-1/2}\left\|\operatorname*{div}\nolimits_{\Gamma}\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1}(\Gamma)}\right).

Inserting (
3.17
), (
3.18
), (
3.19
)
in (
3.20
), we get

\left\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}\left(\Omega\right)}\leq C\left|k\right|\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.21)

Step 5
. Let
\mathbf{R}_{2}
and
\mathbf{K}_{2}
be as in
Lemma
2.6
and consider

\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}:=\mathbf{r}_{0}-\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\quad\text{for\quad}\mathbf{r}_{0}:={\mathbf{R}}_{2}(\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}).

(3.22)

Since
\mathbf{u}_{0}\in\mathbf{X}_{\operatorname*{imp}}
, we have

\mathbf{u}_{0}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)
, and the relation

\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}-k^{2}\mathbf{u}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
implies
\operatorname*{curl}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\in\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)
. Hence,
{\mathbf{r}}_{0}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
together with the
k
-explicit bound

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbf{r}}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=k^{2}\left\|{\mathbf{R}}_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{0})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left|k\right|^{2}\left\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{u0est1/2})}}{\leq}C\left|k\right|^{3}\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.23)

By the same reasoning and the mapping properties of
{\mathbf{R}}_{2}
, we
obtain

\left\|{\mathbf{r}}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C\left|k\right|^{2}\left\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|.

(3.24)

Furthermore, we compute with Lemma
2.6

\operatorname{curl}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\overset{(\ref{defutilde})}{=}\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{R}_{2}(\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{curl}{\mathbf{u}}_{0})-\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\overset{\text{Lem.~\ref{LemRs}}}{=}-\operatorname{curl}{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\operatorname{curl}{\mathbf{u}}_{0}.

(3.25)

We employ the Helmholtz decomposition of
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}
in the form

\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}=\nabla\varphi+\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}
given in
Lemma
2.9
with
\varphi\in H_{0}^{1}\left(\Omega\right)\cap H^{3/2}(\Omega)
,
\mathbf{z\in H}^{1}(\Omega)
, and
\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{z}=0
. Since

\operatorname*{div}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}=\operatorname*{div}\mathbf{r}_{0}

the function
\varphi
does not depend on
\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}

(see (
2.38
)), and we obtain from (
2.37a
)

\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{3/2}(\Omega)}\overset{\text{(\ref{HelmDecompVar1estc})}}{\leq}C\left\|{\mathbf{r}}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}\left(\Omega\right)}\overset{\text{(\ref{Rcurlcurlu0})}}{\leq}C\left|k\right|^{3}\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}.

(3.26)

Next, we estimate
\mathbf{z}
. The definition of
\mathbf{r}_{0}
in
(
3.22
) gives

\gamma_{T}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}=\gamma_{T}{\mathbf{r}}_{0}-\gamma_{T}\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}=\gamma_{T}\mathbf{r}_{0}-\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}+\operatorname*{i}k(\mathbf{u}_{0})_{T}\in\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma).

(3.27)

Lemma
2.9
then implies

\displaystyle\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:rep-curlutilde})}{=}C\left(\left\|\operatorname{curl}{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\left(\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)\right\|+\left\|\gamma_{T}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{gammaTutilderep}), \text{Lem.~\ref{LemRs}}}}{\leq}C\left(\left\|\mathbf{u}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{-1/2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{r}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\left|k\right|\left\|\left(\mathbf{u}_{0}\right)_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}+\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{u0est1/2}), (\ref{estu0T})}}{\leq}C\left(\left|k\right|\left\|{\mathbf{\tilde{g}}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{r}_{0}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right).

(3.28)

Step 6.
The combination of Step 5 with a trace inequality leads to

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\operatorname*{curl}\mathbf{z}\right\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{phiest3/2}), (\ref{curlz1/2})}}{\leq}C\left(\left|k\right|^{3}\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{g}}_{T}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}\left(\Gamma\right)}+\left\|\gamma_{T}\mathbf{r}_{0}\right\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\right).

(3.29)

Let
\mathbf{B}_{2,1}^{1/2}(\Omega)
denote the Besov space as defined, e.g.,
in
[
Tri95
]
. Then the trace map
\displaystyle\gamma_{T}:\mathbf{B}_{2,1}^{1/2}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbf{L}_{T}^{2}(\Gamma)
is a
